## ->TO THE READER<-

**M**Y dear Reader,

Certain pious persons <note>Likely referring to the nuns or associates of Port-Royal, where Arnauld d'Andilly resided.</note> who hold a very particular devotion to Saint Teresa, having desired from me the translation of these three small Treatises: I have undertaken it all the more willingly as I have always revered this great Saint as one of the purest and most brilliant lights that God has given to His Church in these recent centuries. <note>St. Teresa of Avila (1515–1582) was canonized in 1622; by 1659, she was a cornerstone of Catholic Reform spirituality.</note>

The first of these Treatises
                                 A ij

<summary>This page contains the opening of the translator's preface, where Arnauld d'Andilly explains that he translated these three works of St. Teresa at the request of devout friends and out of his own deep lifelong admiration for her.</summary>
<keywords>preface, translator's note, Arnauld d'Andilly, Saint Teresa of Avila, 17th-century piety, Catholic Reform</keywords>

4   ## ->TO THE READER<-

contains seven Meditations on the Our Father <note>original: "le Pater," referring to the Lord's Prayer. St. Teresa’s meditations on this prayer are often drawn from her book *The Way of Perfection*.</note>, arranged for each day of the week, which are so full of the Spirit of God, and so suited to inspire the love of prayer and the sentiments of true piety, that many people have desired, for these very reasons, to have it in a small volume, in order to use it as a manual for the holy and spiritual life. <note>In the 17th century, "handbooks" or "manuals" were popular portable books designed for private, daily prayer and reflection.</note>

The second Treatise contains seventeen Meditations, which the same Saint wrote immediately after her Communions <note>The reception of the Eucharist, which for St. Teresa was frequently a time of intense mystical experience and "ecstasy."</note>, in which one can see a marvelous image of the depths of her heart, her ardent love for God and for neighbor, and an ex-

<summary>Arnauld d'Andilly describes the first two treatises: a week-long series of meditations on the Lord's Prayer and seventeen reflections written by St. Teresa after receiving Communion.</summary>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's preface "To the Reader" by Robert Arnauld d'Andilly. It concludes the description of the second treatise regarding the "dismal state" of sin and introduces the third treatise, consisting of St. Teresa's maxims.</meta>

->TO THE READER<- 5

...cellent representation of the dismal state to which sin reduces us.

The third contains several counsels that she gave to her Religious <note>original: "Religieuses," referring specifically to the nuns of the Discalced Carmelite order which St. Teresa founded.</note>, which are like so many short sentences <note>The term "sentences" refers to "maxims" or "aphorisms"—concise, pithy statements of moral or spiritual truth.</note> full of meaning, which contain in few words the principal rules of Christian piety.

It is unnecessary, my dear Reader, to recommend to you further these little Treatises. All those who have any feeling of piety will not be able to read them without esteeming them. And they will be truly happy if, in imitation of this great Saint, who drew these thoughts rather from her heart than from her mind, they use them more...

A iii <note>This is a signature mark, a code used by early modern printers to keep the folded sheets of the book in the correct order for binding.</note>

<summary>The preface concludes its summary of the three treatises, highlighting a collection of St. Teresa’s maxims and urging the reader to value these works as products of the Saint's heart and spiritual experience.</summary>
<keywords>maxims, counsels, Christian piety, St. Teresa of Avila, devotional literature, religious life</keywords>

8          MEDITATIONS
from Leviticus,
<note>Reference to Leviticus 6:12–13: "The fire on the altar must be kept burning on it; it must not go out. Every morning the priest is to add firewood."</note>
that to pre-
vent the fire of the altar from
being extinguished, God commands the
Priests to put new wood on it
every day: as if He had
wished to signify by this figure
<note>In 17th-century theology, a "figure" is a symbolic representation or "type" from the Old Testament that foreshadows a spiritual truth in the New Testament.</note>
that so the fire of devotion
does not cool and is not ex-
tinguished in us, we must
every day maintain and animate
it by new and lively con-
siderations. And although it
might seem at first that there
is some imperfection in this
<note>The "imperfection" refers to the human need for constant variety and new thoughts, rather than being able to rest eternally in a single truth.</note>
it is nevertheless a conduct of
divine providence, which causes
our soul, following its natural
inclination, to occupy itself
without ceasing in the search for
God’s infinite perfections,
without being able to content
itself with less than this
object which has no boundaries,
because He alone is capable of
filling it.

<summary>St. Teresa uses the biblical imagery of the perpetual fire on the altar of Leviticus to explain that the human soul requires daily "fuel" through new spiritual reflections to keep its devotion from fading. She argues that the soul's restless search for new insights is a divine design intended to lead it toward the infinite nature of God.</summary>
<keywords>Leviticus, altar fire, spiritual devotion, divine providence, infinite perfections of God, meditation, religious symbolism</keywords>

### ON THE LORD'S PRAYER. 9
<note>original: "SUR LE PATER." The title refers to the *Pater Noster* or the Lord's Prayer.</note>
Just as, then, the love of God
is the divine fire that we in-
tend to maintain within our souls,
it requires for this much
wood, and every day one must 
add something new to it; because the
heat of our will is so
active that it consumes it
entirely, and no matter
what quantity there may be, the will 
always finds it to be too little,
until—upon entering into the perfect
possession of this infinite Good, which
alone is capable of satisfying
it fully—this same fire of 
love that it will have maintained
within itself here below, 
<note>"Here below" is a common 17th-century idiom for earthly, mortal life as opposed to the heavenly afterlife.</note>
becomes in heaven its divine and its eternal-
ly nourishing food.
Now, as one can say that
the Lord’s Prayer is the wood
most suitable for maintaining this
fire of divine love, it has
seemed to me that, to prevent the lo-
<note>The text cuts off mid-word ("l'a-"), likely intending "l'amour" (love) or "l'ardeur" (ardor), to be continued on the following page.</note>

<summary>The author explains that the soul’s love for God is a fire that must be daily fueled with new meditations to satisfy the restless human will, eventually becoming eternal nourishment in heaven. He identifies the Lord's Prayer as the most effective fuel for maintaining this divine ardor.</summary>

->10      MEDITATIONS<-

[the soul] does not grow lukewarm <note>original: "s’attiediffe" (s'attiédisse). In 17th-century devotional literature, spiritual "lukewarmness" or tepidity was a feared state of indifference or lack of zeal in one's religious duties.</note> through the repeti-
tion so frequent of this holy
prayer, it would not be out of
place to seek some means,
to ensure that in reciting it every
day, we might always con-
ceive new thoughts
to maintain our spirit
and our will in a vi-
gor always new. This
will be done without difficulty by divi-
ding the seven petitions <note>Traditional Christian exegesis divides the "Our Father" into seven distinct requests or "petitions" (e.g., "Hallowed be thy name," "Thy kingdom come," etc.).</note> which
are contained therein according to the seven
days of the week, so that
each day has its own; and by
giving on each of these days to
God a particular name, which
includes all that we de-
sire and hope to obtain from
him through this petition.
    It is well known what these
petitions are. And as for the names
that one can give to God,
we will take those of Father,

<summary>To prevent the Lord's Prayer from becoming a mindless routine, the author suggests a weekly meditation cycle where one of the prayer's seven petitions is focused on each day. This method involves addressing God by a specific name each day to keep the practitioner's spiritual "vigor" and will engaged.</summary>
<keywords>Lord's Prayer, spiritual lukewarmness, petitions, meditation method, spiritual vigor, daily prayer, names of God</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of divine names introduced at the bottom of page 10. It completes the instructional guide on how to assign a specific attribute of God to each petition of the Lord's Prayer according to the days of the week.</meta>

->ON THE LORD’S PRAYER. 11<-

King, Spouse, Shepherd, Redeemer, Physician, and Judge: In such a way that each person will awaken <note>original: "réueillera" (réveillera). In this context, it means to alert or sharpen one's mental focus during prayer.</note> their attention, and stir themselves more and more to love him by saying:
On Monday: *Our Father who art in the Heavens, hallowed be thy name.*
On Tuesday: *Our King, thy kingdom come.*
On Wednesday: *Spouse of my soul <note>The title "Spouse" (Epoux) reflects a tradition in mystical theology where the relationship between God and the human soul is described in terms of a spiritual marriage.</note>, thy will be done.*
On Thursday: *Our Shepherd, give us today the bread of which we have need each day.*
On Friday: *Our Redeemer, forgive us our offenses as we forgive those who have offended us.*
On Saturday: *Our Physician <note>In devotional literature, sin was often likened to a disease, making God the "Great Physician" who heals the soul.</note>, do not let us succumb <note>original: "succomber." While modern English versions usually say "lead us not into temptation," this French version emphasizes the desire not to "yield" or "fall" to it.</note> to temptation.*
And on Sunday: *Our Judge, deliver us from evil.*

### FIRST PETITION.

#### For Monday.

*Our Father who art in*
*the heavens.*

ALthough the name of Father <note>In the Christian tradition, the title "Father" emphasizes a familial relationship based on grace, rather than a distant creator/subject dynamic.</note> is the one that best suits all these petitions, and which gives us the most confidence in obtaining what we ask of God—inasmuch as it is by this name that He wished to bind Himself <note>original: "s'obliger." The author suggests that by inviting the faithful to call Him "Father," God takes on a parental obligation to hear and provide for His children.</note> to grant it to us—it is nevertheless not a violation of His order and His holy will to add the others <note>Referring to the secondary titles mentioned on the previous page, such as King, Spouse, and Physician.</note>; for besides the fact that they all belong to Him with such truth, they serve to excite our devotion, and to add, as it were, new wood <note>A common spiritual metaphor: just as wood fuels a fire, different names and attributes of God fuel the warmth and intensity of the soul's prayer.</note>

<summary>This page begins the specific meditation for Monday, explaining why the title "Father" is used for the first petition of the Lord's Prayer. The author argues that while "Father" is the most fitting name to inspire confidence in God's mercy, using other divine titles helps to further kindle the believer's fervor.</summary>
<keywords>First Petition, Monday meditation, Fatherhood of God, devotion, prayer titles, spiritual metaphors, divine will</keywords>

Let us consider how the physicians of the earth act. They only go to see those who send for them; and it is not the most sick whom they visit most often, but those who pay them the best. They represent the illness as being greater than it is, and sometimes even prolong it, in order to earn more. <note>In the 1600s, medical professionals were often the subject of satire (most famously by the playwright Molière) for their perceived greed and ineffective, expensive treatments.</note> They treat the poor based on the reports of others, but the rich in person. ->ON THE LORD'S PRAYER <note>original: "SVR LE PATER." The "Pater" refers to the *Pater Noster* or the "Our Father."</note>. 77<-
***
SEVENTH PETITION.

For Sunday.

*Deliver us from evil.*

**W**HEN, by this seventh and final petition, we pray to God to deliver us from evil; we do not specify in particular from which evil we ask Him to deliver us. But we pray to Him only to deliver us from all that is properly and truly evil; that is to say, from all that can cause us to lose the blessings of grace, or of glory <note>In this context, "glory" (la gloire) refers to the state of eternal blessedness in Heaven.</note>. Among these evils, there are some which are properly penalties and punishments, such as temp- <note>The word is truncated as it reaches the end of the page; it likely continues as "tentations" (temptations) on the following page.</note>

G iij <note>A signature mark used by early modern printers to ensure the pages were bound in the correct order.</note>

<meta>This page continues the meditation on the Seventh Petition ("Deliver us from evil") from page 77. The previous page ended mid-word with "temp-", which concludes here as "tations."</meta>

78      MEDITATIONS

tations, illnesses, afflictions, grievances that affect one's honor, and other similar things. But these cannot properly be called "evils," except insofar as they serve as an occasion to fall into sin. For this same reason, riches, honors, and all temporal goods <note>The term "temporal goods" refers to worldly possessions, status, or wealth that exist only within the limits of time and physical life, as opposed to eternal or spiritual blessings.</note> can rightly be called "evils"; because they often also provide us with an occasion to offend God. 

Thus, we ask to be delivered not only from all these evils, but also from all those goods which might cause us to fall into eternal damnation. And because it properly belongs to the Sovereign Judge to free us from these punishments, the title of "Judge" is very fitting for God in this instance <note>The author suggests that when we pray to be delivered from the "evil" of punishment or sin, we are addressing God in His specific role as the one who holds the power of final judgment.</note>.

<summary>The text clarifies that physical suffering or worldly success are not inherent evils or goods; rather, they are defined by whether they lead a person to sin or to God. It concludes by noting that God is addressed as a "Judge" when we ask for deliverance from these spiritual dangers.</summary>

<keywords>Lord's Prayer, Seventh Petition, sin, temporal goods, Sovereign Judge, eternal damnation, spiritual discernment, 17th-century French devotion</keywords>

of my God, how different your effects are from those produced by the love of the world! This latter love <note>The author contrasts *amor mundi* (love of the world/selfish love) with *caritas* (divine love/charity).</note> desires no company, because it seems to it that company separates it from the person it loves. ### AFTER COMMUNION. 159

-solation, what shall I do to satisfy you? All the services that I can render you, even if I were to render you many, are defective and miserable. Wait then, O my soul, wait with patience; since you know neither the day nor the hour <note>A scriptural allusion to Matthew 25:13: "Watch therefore, for ye know neither the day nor the hour wherein the Son of man cometh." # ADVICE
# OF ST. TERESA
### FOR
### HER NUNS.

1. <note>Large decorative initial L</note> **The spirit** of man resembles the earth, which, although fertile, nevertheless produces only thorns and brambles when it is not cultivated. <note>St. Teresa of Avila (1515–1582) frequently used agricultural metaphors to describe the interior life and the "garden" of the soul.</note>

2. Speak advantageously of all spiritual persons: such as the religious <note>Referring to members of religious orders, such as monks or friars.</note>, priests, and hermits.

3. When you are with many others, always speak little.

4. Conduct yourself with great modesty <note>In this context, modesty refers to a combination of humility, decorum, and outward self-control.</note> in all the things you do and in all the matters you handle.

5. Never dispute much—

6. Speak to everyone with a moderate cheerfulness.

7. Never make fun of anything whatsoever. <note>The original French *raillerie* implies mocking or derisive jesting, which the Saint viewed as a distraction from spiritual gravity and charity.</note>

8. Never rebuke anyone except with discretion and humility, and with a secret shame <note>original: "confuſion"; in this context, it refers to the internal embarrassment one feels when recognizing their own unworthiness while correcting others.</note> for your own particular failings.

9. Always accommodate yourself to the temperament <note>original: "l'humeur"; referring to the disposition or mood of others.</note> of the people with whom you deal. Be cheerful with those who are cheerful, and sad with those who are sad: and finally, make yourself all things to all people, in order to win them all. <note>This is a direct reference to the missionary strategy of St. Paul in 1 Corinthians 9:22.</note>

10. Never speak without having thought well upon it beforehand, and having strongly recommended it to our Lord, so as to say nothing that might be disagreeable to Him.

11. Never make excuses for yourself, unless there is a great reason to do so.

68. Remember that you have but one soul; that you will die but once; that you have but one life, which is short; and that there is but one glory, which is eternal. And this thought will detach you from many things.

69. Let your desire be to see God; your fear, that you might lose Him; your sorrow, that you do not yet possess Him; and your joy, that He can draw you to Himself. And you will live in great peace.

<meta>This page follows the conclusion of Saint Teresa’s Maxims. It provides the legal authorization (Privilege) for the publication of Arnauld d’Andilly’s translations, specifically identifying this volume as his translation of the Meditations of Saint Teresa.</meta>

### Extract of the Royal Privilege.

By the grace and Privilege of the King, given at Compiègne on the 27th day of August in the year of grace 1652. Permission is granted to <term>Sieur</term> <note>A title of respect, similar to "Lord" or "Sir," used for gentlemen of high social standing.</note> Arnauld d’Andilly, Councilor to His Majesty in his State and <term>Privy Councils</term> <note>The private circle of advisors to the King of France.</note>, to have printed the Translation he has made of St. John Climacus, and all the Translations he shall make hereafter of the works of the Holy Fathers of the Church, and other Ecclesiastical Authors, during the span of twenty years. This comes with very express prohibitions to all persons, of whatever quality and condition they may be, from printing, selling, or distributing any part of them in any place under the obedience of His Majesty, without the consent of the said Sieur d’Andilly, or of those who shall hold his rights, under penalty of confiscation of the copies, payment of all costs, damages, and interests, and a fine of three thousand <term>livres</term> <note>The primary currency of France at the time; 3,000 livres was a very substantial fine, equivalent to many years of wages for a skilled laborer.</note>, as is set forth at greater length by the said Privilege. Signed, by the King in his Council.

BERAULD.

*And the said Sieur Arnauld d’Andilly has transferred his right of Privilege for the present Treatise of the Meditations of Saint Teresa to Pierre le Petit, ordinary Printer and Bookseller to the King, to enjoy its use, following the agreement made between the said Sieur d’Andilly and him.*

*Finished printing for the first time the 1st day of March 1655.*

<summary>This page is a legal "Privilege," a 17th-century precursor to copyright, granting Arnauld d'Andilly exclusive rights to publish his translations of religious works for twenty years. It specifically notes the transfer of these rights for Saint Teresa's Meditations to the royal printer Pierre le Petit.</summary>
<keywords>Royal Privilege, Arnauld d'Andilly, Pierre le Petit, copyright law, 17th-century publishing, Saint Teresa of Avila, Louis XIV era</keywords>

->TERESA OF JESUS.<-

**A**s our Maker knows how we are fashioned <note>original: "nuestra hechura," referring to our human nature and design</note>, and understands that because the capacity of our soul is infinite, it seeks new things every day and is not content with receiving only one, the Lord Himself commands in the sixth chapter of Leviticus <note>Refers to Leviticus 6:12-13, which mandates that the fire on the altar must be kept burning and never go out.</note> that—to prevent the fire of the altar from being extinguished—every day

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening meditation on the "Our Father," completing the biblical metaphor from Leviticus 6 regarding the altar fire. The previous page ended with the introduction of the priest's duty to maintain the flame.</meta>

4 MEDITATIONS

the priest should fuel it <note>original: "le ceuasse," literally "to bait" or "to feed," referring here to the stoking of a fire</note> with fresh wood. This serves as a symbolic figure <note>original: "sinificando en figura," a common theological term meaning an Old Testament event that foreshadows a New Testament truth</note> to show that, so the warmth of devotion does not die out or grow cold, we must feed it every day with new and vibrant reflections. 

And although this constant need might seem like an imperfection, it is actually a matter of divine providence. It ensures that the soul, following its own nature, continues to seek out the infinite perfections of God and is never satisfied with anything less—for only He can truly fill its capacity.

There is only one thing we seek to sustain, and that is the fire of the love of God. However, many logs are necessary, and they must be renewed every day, because the heat and the intensity of our own will consumes everything. To the soul, everything seems insufficient until it reaches the point where it can feed upon that same infinite fire, which alone satisfies and fills it completely.

<summary>Saint Teresa explains that the soul requires daily "fuel" through new meditations to keep the fire of divine love burning, arguing that our natural restlessness is a gift that leads us toward God's infinity.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, spiritual discipline, meditation, soul's capacity, Leviticus, divine providence, Saint Teresa of Avila</keywords>

# ON THE OUR FATHER. 5

our capacity. Since the prayer of the Our Father <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> is the most ready firewood <note>The author continues the metaphor from the previous page, where "wood" represents the themes of meditation that sustain the "fire" of devotion.</note> to sustain this divine fire alive, so that the will does not grow lukewarm through frequent repetition, it seems reasonable to seek a way to repeat it every day so that it refreshes our understanding with new considerations, and simultaneously sustains the fire and heat within our will.

This will be conveniently accomplished by distributing the seven petitions of the prayer across the seven days of the week. Each day, we shall take one petition under a different title and name that suits it, to which we may bring together everything we seek in that petition, and all that exists in everything we desire to obtain from God.

The petitions themselves are already well known.

->a iii<- <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to keep the pages of a book in the correct order during binding.</note>

<summary>The author proposes a weekly meditation structure for the Lord's Prayer, assigning one of its seven petitions to each day of the week to keep the practitioner's devotion fervent and fresh.</summary>

# 6 MEDITATIONS

These are the titles and names of God: Father, King, Spouse, Shepherd, Redeemer, Physician, and Judge.

In such a way that on Monday, each person may awaken saying: Our Father who art in heaven, hallowed be thy name. 

On Tuesday: Our King, thy kingdom come to us. 

On Wednesday: Spouse of my soul, thy will be done. 

On Thursday: Our Shepherd, give us this day our daily bread. 

On Friday: Our Redeemer, forgive us our debts, even as we forgive our debtors. 

On Saturday: Our Physician, let us not fall into temptation. 

On Sunday: Our Judge, deliver us from evil.

<note>A decorative ornament in the shape of a cross is centered on the page, composed of various flourishes and floral motifs.</note>

<summary>This page assigns a specific name of God and a corresponding petition from the Lord's Prayer to each day of the week, ranging from "Father" on Monday to "Judge" on Sunday.</summary>
<keywords>meditation, names of God, Lord's Prayer, weekly devotion, liturgy, spiritual discipline, Spanish mysticism</keywords>

# ON THE OUR FATHER <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note>. 9

And in particular, let a person consider and say: "How good is this Father to me?" for He willed that I should have existence and enjoy this **dignity** <note>In this context, it refers to the high spiritual status or honor of divine adoption—being considered a child of God.</note> of being His child, leaving aside the creation of other men who might have been better than I; pondering here how much this Father deserves to be loved and served, who by His goodness alone created all things for me, and created me so that I might serve Him and enjoy Him.

28 ->MEDITATIONS<-

Shepherd, to whom it belongs to feed his flock, giving us our daily bread: because it suits the Father, King, and Spouse <note>In mystical theology, God is often described as the "Spouse" of the soul or the Church, a relationship defined by intimacy and mutual commitment.</note> very well to be a Shepherd; and by natural right we—His children, vassals <note>The author uses the language of the Spanish Golden Age, where a "vassal" owed loyalty to a King, mirroring the soul's loyalty to God.</note>, and spouses—can ask that He maintain and feed us with delicacies according to His majesty and our own greatness, for we are His children. I cannot convince myself that in this petition we are asking for a temporal <note>Temporal refers to worldly, physical, or time-bound things, as opposed to eternal or spiritual things.</note> thing for the sustenance of the physical life, but rather a spiritual thing for the sustenance of the soul; because of the seven petitions <note>Following the tradition of Saint Augustine, the "Our Father" is traditionally divided into seven distinct requests: three concerning God’s glory and four concerning human needs.</note> we ask here, the first three are for God: the sanctification of His name—

<summary>This page argues that the "daily bread" requested in the Lord's Prayer is primarily spiritual nourishment for the soul rather than physical food, emphasizing the believer's right as a child of God to claim these "goods."</summary>

# ON THE OUR FATHER. 29

...name, His kingdom, and His will; and of the four petitions that we ask for ourselves, this is the first, in which alone we ask that He **give** to us: because in the others we ask that He **take away** our sins, our temptations, and all evil. Now, since there is only one thing we ask our Father to give us, it must not be a temporal <note>Temporal refers to physical, worldly things that exist within time, as opposed to eternal, spiritual realities.</note> thing for the body; furthermore, it is not fitting for the children of such a Father to ask for things so lowly and common—things He gives even to inferior creatures and to man without them even asking. This is especially true since His Majesty <note>In the Spanish Golden Age, "His Majesty" was a standard way to refer to God, mirroring the language used for earthly kings.</note> has advised us to ask of Him by seeking first the things of His kingdom, which is what concerns our souls; for His Majesty takes care of the rest. For this reason, He declared through Saint Matthew: *Give us this day our supersubstantial bread* <note>original: "El pan nuestro sobrefustancial". While most modern Bibles say "daily bread," the author is citing the Latin Vulgate's *panem supersubstantialem*. ### ON THE OUR FATHER. 37

...the mystery of the most holy Sacrament, the excellence of this food <note>original: "manjar." To deify or divinize means to make divine. The author is making a bold theological claim that while the Incarnation divinized Jesus’s humanity, the Eucharist divinizes the believer.</note> more than his own soul and his flesh, uniting them with his person: but in this Sacrament, God wished to deify all men, who are best sustained by the foods with which they were raised as children: and as we were fathered in baptism by the whole of God, he wished that we should be sustained by the whole of him, in accordance with the dignity of children that he gave us.

44 MEDITATIONS

Wise man: how is it possible that a man does not forgive his brother, yet asks forgiveness from God? <note>Original: "Sabio". This is a reference to the "Wise Man" of the Old Testament, specifically the author of the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 28:1-4.</note> He who desires to take vengeance, God will take vengeance upon him, and will hold his sins without remission. The subject matter of this petition is most general, and embraces infinite things; because the debts are beyond counting, the redemption is most abundant, and the price of forgiveness is infinite, which is the death and passion of Christ. 

Here one must recall or bring to memory one's own sins, and those of the whole world: the gravity of a mortal sin, which, by being an offense against God, cannot be redeemed or paid for by another <note>In early modern theology, because God is considered an infinite being, a "mortal sin" against Him was seen as having infinite weight, requiring more than mere human effort to reconcile.</note>: the restoration for so many offenses committed against such great and infinite majesty and goodness. We owe God love, and fear, and supreme reverence, for being who He is: we owe Him the of— <note>The text breaks off mid-word; likely "ofensas" (offenses) or "obligaciones" (obligations).</note>

—fenses that we commit against him in return for this; for we ask him to release us from all these debts when we ask him to forgive us our debts. In the execution of this work lie all his riches and all our good fortune; for he is the offended one, the Redeemer, and the ransom. <note>The author highlights a central tenet of Christian "substitutionary atonement": that God, through Christ, provides the payment (the "ransom") for the very offenses committed against Himself.</note>

For today, there is no need to point out a specific location or particular step of his Passion, for the entirety of it is the work of our redemption. 46      MEDITACIONES

—goodness and beauty, ingratitude and forgetfulness of my benefits, and hardness toward my inspirations: for once you have removed these three, I remain fastened by another three, which are: infinite love, gratitude for the blessings that my Father gives you through me, and a tenderness of heart <note>The Spanish "blandura de entrañas" literally means "softness of the entrails," a common Early Modern idiom for deep compassion or emotional vulnerability.</note> to receive you.

This day is one of great silence, and of some particular austerity and mortification, and of remembering the Saints to whom we are devoted, through whose intercession we shall also obtain the forgiveness we ask of God. On this day, special prayer must be made for those who are in mortal sin <note>In Catholic theology, a "mortal sin" is a grave offense that separates the soul from God's grace; praying for the conversion of such individuals was a standard devotional practice.</note>, and for those who wish us ill or have wished us ill, and have caused us some offense.

<note>A decorative floral ornament, often used by printers of this era to fill space or mark the conclusion of a chapter.</note>

->SIXTH PETITION.<-

->FOR SATURDAY.<-

*And lead us not into temptation.* <note>The original Spanish text uses "Y no nos dexes caer en la tentacion," which translates literally as "And do not let us fall into temptation," a phrasing common in early modern Spanish devotion.</note>

Since our enemies are such, and so persistent <note>The Spanish "importunos" suggests enemies who are relentless or trouble us at inconvenient times.</note>, they always place us in difficulty; and as our weakness is so great, we are prone to falling if the Almighty does not help us. Therefore, it is necessary that we be persevering in asking favor from our Lord, so that He does not permit us to be overcome by present temptations, nor allow us to return to our past sins.

We do not ask Him that He not permit us to be tempted, but rather that...

48 MEDITATIONS

...not to be overcome by temptations; for since temptation, when overcome by His favor and our will, is for His glory and our crown, His Majesty <note>This honorific (Spanish: "su Magestad") was frequently used by Spanish mystics and devotional writers like St. Teresa of Ávila to refer to God.</note> commands us to ask it with these words: *And lead us not into temptation*: so that we may understand that being tempted happens by His permission, being overcome is due to our own frailty, and the victory is His alone.

Let us then consider here how it is true that we are all weak, sick, and wounded—both because we inherited this condition from our parents <note>A reference to the "stain" of Original Sin inherited from the first parents, Adam and Eve.</note> and because we ourselves, through our own sins and past bad habits, have weakened ourselves further, wounded from head to toe. Let us present ourselves in this way before this heavenly Physician <note>A traditional metaphor for Christ, emphasizing His role in healing the soul rather than just judging it.</note>, and let us beg Him not to let us fall into temptation, holding us with His powerful hand...

<summary>This page concludes the reasoning for the Sixth Petition, explaining that while God permits temptation, victory over it comes from His grace. ### ON THE OUR FATHER <note>original: "PATER NOSTER"</note>. 49

This title of Physician is very pleasing to His Divine Majesty <note>This honorific, "su divina magestad," was a standard way for Spanish writers of this period to refer to Christ or God the Father.</note>, and it was the office that He most practiced while living in this world, healing those with incurable bodily illnesses and souls burdened with long-standing vices. His Majesty performed this office for mankind by comparing Himself to the Samaritan, who with oil and wine healed the man whom the thieves had robbed, wounded, and left half-dead. <note>A reference to the Parable of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10), traditionally interpreted in the Middle Ages and Renaissance as an allegory for Christ saving humanity.</note> Physician and Redeemer are one and the same thing; except that the Redeemer has regard for past sins, as Saint Paul said, and the Physician for healing present wounds and illnesses, and all future faults.

54 MEDITATIONS

When the sick person does not wish to take what they are prescribed, and does not refrain from what they are forbidden, the physician usually leaves them, unless the sick person is delirious <note>original: "frenetico." In a historical medical context, this refers to a patient suffering from "phrenitis"—a state of delirium or temporary insanity caused by fever. The author suggests that Christ treats sinners as if they are not in their right mind rather than simply rebellious.</note>. But this, our sovereign Physician, neither abandons those who are poorly governed, nor the disobedient; He cures them all as if they were delirious, seeking a thousand ways to bring them back to their senses.

On this day, it is appropriate to bring to memory the burial of the Lord, and to consider those five fountains of His wounds, which are and shall remain open until the general resurrection, for the healing of all of ours <note>The "five fountains" refers to the Five Holy Wounds inflicted during the Crucifixion (the hands, feet, and side), which in mystical tradition are viewed as inexhaustible sources of grace and healing.</note>. And since we are healed by them, let us endeavor to anoint them lovingly and charitably with the ointment of mortification <note><term>Mortification</term>: The practice of self-denial or asceticism to overcome sin and bring the will into subjection to God.</note>, humility, patience, and meekness, occupying ourselves in the good of our neighbors: for we cannot have Him...

<summary>The author explains that Christ, unlike earthly doctors, does not abandon even the most disobedient "patients," treating spiritual waywardness as a form of delirium. It concludes by exhorting the reader to meditate on Christ's five wounds as fountains of healing, to be "anointed" by the reader's own virtues.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Physician, delirium, Five Wounds, mortification, spiritual healing, compassion of Christ</keywords>

This manner of praying pleases the Lord greatly; for just as great lords delight in hearing common folk <note>original: "rusticos." Refers to simple, unrefined people or peasants whose speech is direct and unpretentious.</note> who ask them for something roughly and simply, so the Lord receives much pleasure when we pray to him with such haste <note>original: "priessa." ...pity for the time that I did not live in sorrow <note>Teresa refers to her "lost years" before her deeper conversion, grieving that she did not spend that time in the "sorrow" of longing for God.</note>. O Lord, how gentle are your paths! But who can walk them without fear? I fear being without serving You; and when I set out to serve You, I find nothing that satisfies me or that can repay even a small part of what I owe. It seems I would wish to employ myself entirely in this service; and when I carefully consider my misery <note>In mystical writing, "misery" refers to the inherent weakness and nothingness of the human condition without God’s grace.</note>, I see that I can do nothing good, unless You give it to me.

O my God and my mercy, what shall I do so that I do not undo the great things You do within me? II.

Many times, my Lord, I consider that if there is anything that can sustain living without You, it is in solitude; for there the soul rests in its own rest. Yet, because the soul does not enjoy this rest with complete liberty, the torment is often doubled. But compared to the pain that comes from having to deal with creatures <note>In 16th-century spiritual writing, "creatures" (*criaturas*) refers to all created things—especially other human beings—which were often viewed as distractions from a pure focus on the Divine.</note> and the soul ceasing to be alone with her Creator, this solitude is held to be a delight. But what is this, my God? Why does this rest cause such [longing] in the soul that only seeks to please You? O powerful love of God, how different Your effects are from the love of the world! This [worldly love] desires no company, because it seems to it that others will take away what it possesses... <note>The text cuts off here as it reaches the end of the page.</note>

<summary>Saint Teresa reflects on the paradox of solitude, noting that while being alone is the only way to endure life without God's immediate presence, it also increases the soul's longing for Him. She contrasts the divine love that seeks solitude for spiritual union with worldly love that avoids others out of possessiveness.</summary>
<keywords>solitude, divine love vs. worldly love, creatures, spiritual rest, Exclamations of the Soul, Saint Teresa of Avila</keywords>

### OF THE SOUL TO GOD. 67

...it possesses. That [love] of my God, the more lovers it understands there to be, the more it grows; and thus its joys are tempered <note>Spanish: *se tiemplan*. St. Teresa suggests that the soul’s spiritual joy is moderated or flavored with a certain sadness because it realizes this joy is not shared by everyone.</note> by seeing that not everyone enjoys that [same] Good.

O my Good, this causes it to be that even in the greatest delights and pleasures <note>Spanish: *regalos y contentos*. These terms specifically refer to "spiritual favors" or mystical consolations—the emotional and spiritual "gifts" God grants to the soul during deep prayer.</note> one has with You, the memory of the many who do not want these pleasures, and of those who are to lose them forever, brings pain. And thus the soul seeks ways to find company, and willingly leaves its own joy when it thinks it might play some part in helping others strive to enjoy it. 

But, my heavenly Father, would it not be better to leave these desires for when the soul has fewer of Your delights, and for now employ itself entirely in enjoying You? O my Jesus! How great is the love You have for the children of men <note>A common biblical phrase (from the Latin *filiis hominum*) referring to all of humanity.</note>, that the—

f ij <note>This is a printer's signature mark, used by early modern binders to keep the pages of a book in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Saint Teresa describes how divine love, unlike worldly love, grows when shared. She expresses a "painful joy" where her own spiritual delights are marked by a deep desire for others to experience the same salvation.</summary>

->68 EXCLAMATIONS<-

...greatest service that can be rendered to You is to leave You for their love and benefit <note>Spanish: *ganancia*. Teresa describes the spiritual paradox where leaving the "sweetness" of private prayer to serve others results in a greater spiritual "profit."</note>; and then You are possessed even more entirely. For although the soul’s will is not so satisfied in its own personal enjoyment, the soul rejoices that it pleases You, and it sees that the joys of the earth are uncertain (even if they seem to be given by You while we live in this mortal state) unless they are accompanied by love for one's neighbor. Whoever does not love his neighbor does not love You, my Lord, since we see shown, with so much blood <note>A reference to the Crucifixion and the blood shed by Christ for humanity.</note>, the immense love You have for the children of Adam <note>Original: *hijos de Adan*. ->OF THE SOUL TO GOD. 69<-

...labors and sorrows Your Son earned it <note>Original: *trabajos y dolores*. Teresa refers to the "glory" mentioned on the previous page, which was purchased through Christ's suffering.</note>, and how little we had deserved it, and how much it deserves that the greatness of a love—which has taught us to love at such a high cost—not be met with ingratitude, my soul has been greatly afflicted. Blessed be such a Lord: blessed be such great mercy: and praised be He forever for such compassionate pity.

<summary>Saint Teresa reflects on the high price Christ paid for human salvation and laments the ingratitude and spiritual "frenzy" of humanity. She marvels at God's capacity to offer a helping hand even to those who offend Him.</summary>

->70 EXCLAMATIONS<-

O my soul <note>Original: *O anima mia*. While the text is Spanish, Teresa uses the Latin-inflected "anima" to address her own spirit in a formal, devotional manner.</note>, bless so great a God forever. How can one turn against Him? Oh! For those who are ungrateful, the very greatness of His mercy <note>Original: *merced*. This refers to God's unmerited grace or favor; Teresa warns that rejecting such great love increases one's spiritual peril.</note> turns to their own harm. Remedy this, You, my God. O sons of men, how long will you be hard of heart <note>A scriptural allusion to Psalm 4:2, often used to describe those who are stubborn in sin or indifferent to God.</note>, and keep that heart only to be against this most gentle Jesus? What is this? Will our wickedness perhaps prevail against Him? No, for the life of man comes to an end like the flower of the field <note>Original: *la flor del heno* (the flower of the hay/grass). An allusion to Psalm 103:15, emphasizing the fleeting nature of human existence compared to the eternal.</note>, and the Son of the Virgin must come to deliver that terrible judgment. O my powerful God, since You must judge us even if we do not wish it, why do we not look to what is important for us—to keep You pleased for that hour? But who, who would not want such a just judge? Blessed are those who, in that fearful moment <note>Original: *temeroso punto*. This refers specifically to the moment of death or the Final Judgment.</note>, shall rejoice with You.

<summary>Saint Teresa exhorts the soul to gratitude, lamenting human hard-heartedness toward the "gentle Jesus" and reminding the reader of the transience of life and the certainty of the Final Judgment.</summary>

### OF THE SOUL TO GOD. 71

O my God and my Lord, for the one whom You have raised up, and who has realized how miserably they were lost <note>Original: *miseramente se perdió*. Teresa often speaks of the soul "losing itself" when it turns away from God toward worldly distractions.</note> just to gain a very brief pleasure <note>Original: *un muy breve contento*. This refers to the fleeting nature of sinful or worldly satisfaction compared to eternal joy.</note>, and who is determined to please You always—with Your favor helping them (for You do not fail, O Good of my soul, those who love You, nor do You cease to answer those who call upon You)—what remedy is there, Lord, to be able to live afterward? How can one live without dying from the memory of having lost such great good as they would have possessed while in that <term>innocence</term> <note>Original: *innocencia*. In Catholic theology, this refers to the state of "baptismal innocence," the purity of a soul immediately after the sacrament of baptism, before the commission of any personal sins.</note> that remains from baptism? The best life that such a soul can have is to die continually with this feeling of grief. But the soul that tenderly loves You, how shall it be able to suffer this? But what folly <note>Original: *desatino*. This suggests a state of spiritual confusion or "nonsense" where the speaker realizes they have momentarily lost sight of God's infinite capacity for forgiveness.</note> I ask You, my Lord: it seems I have forgotten Your greatness and Your mercies, and how You came into the world...

<summary>Saint Teresa expresses profound remorse for having traded eternal grace for fleeting pleasures, lamenting the loss of baptismal innocence while clinging to the hope of God's responsiveness to those who love Him.</summary>
<keywords>penitence, baptismal innocence, divine mercy, spiritual grief, Saint Teresa of Ávila</keywords>

...for sinners, and You bought us for so great a price <note>A common theological reference to the "ransom" of Christ's blood paid for the salvation of humanity.</note>, and You paid for our false pleasures by suffering such cruel torments and scourgings. ### IV.

IT seems, my Lord, that my soul finds rest when considering the joy it will have if, through Your mercy, it were granted...

<summary>Teresa meditates on Christ's Passion as the price paid for human sin, specifically how his physical suffering (the blindfolding and the crown of thorns) atones for human spiritual failings. She concludes that the soul's earthly distress can only be fully cured by the Beatific Vision—seeing God face to face.</summary>
<keywords>Passion of Christ, Redemption, Divine Mercy, Crown of Thorns, Spiritual Fatigue, Vision of God</keywords>

...to enjoy You. This term implies actively working to gain something or to win someone over, portraying God as a persistent suitor of the soul.</note> and calling so that I might be wholly employed in You.

You are powerful, Great God; now it can be understood if my soul understands itself, looking at the time it has lost, and how in an instant <note>Original: *en un punto*. ### VI.

O my Delight, <note>Original: *Deleyte*. In 16th-century Spanish mysticism, this refers to the spiritual joy or sweetness found in the presence of the Divine.</note> Lord of all creation, and my God, how long shall I wait to see Your presence? What remedy do You give to one who has so little on this earth to find any rest apart from You? O long life! O painful life! O life that is not lived! <note>Teresa frequently employs the paradox of "living without living," expressing that true life only begins after death when the soul is united with God. This echoes her famous poem: "I live without living in myself... I die because I do not die."</note> O, what lonely solitude, so beyond remedy!

### XI.

In that place, it will be of no use to try to avoid thinking about matters of faith, as it has done here; and the soul sees itself, sees itself being torn away from what it feels it had only just begun...

112 EXCLAMATIONS

Lord, once this battle has begun, whom are they to go and fight but Him who has made Himself the master of this fortress where they used to dwell? This is the highest part of the soul, from which He has cast them out <note>Teresa refers here to the "faculties" mentioned on the previous page—the memory, intellect, and will—which God "displaces" during intense mystical experiences so that the soul can focus entirely on Him.</note> so that they might turn back to conquer their Conqueror. Having grown weary of seeing themselves without Him, they quickly surrender; they spend themselves, losing all their strength, and yet they fight better, for in giving themselves up as conquered, they conquer their Victor. O my soul, what a wondrous battle you have endured in this sorrow, and how literally it all comes to pass! Since my beloved is mine and I am my beloved's <note>original: *mi amado à mi: y yo à mi amado*. A direct reference to the Song of Solomon 2:16, a favorite text of mystics to describe the union of the soul with God.</note>, who shall be the one to step between them to separate or extinguish two such burning fires? It would be labor in vain, for they have already become one.

XVII.

O my God, and my infinite Wisdom, without measure and without limit <note>original: *sin tassa*. In this context, it refers to a bounty that cannot be measured or taxed by human standards.</note>, and above all angelic and human understandings! ### OF THE SOUL TO GOD. 117

...could hope to be able to leave, or to put it better, would not fear finding oneself cast out. But woe is me, Lord, for as long as this mortal life lasts, eternal life is always in danger.

O life, enemy of my well-being! Who might have permission to end you? I endure you because God endures you; I sustain you because you are His. Do not be a traitor to me, nor ungrateful. 

Despite all this, woe is me, my Lord, for my exile <note>original: *destierro*. A common theme in mystic literature, viewing earthly life as a period of forced absence from one's true home in Heaven.</note> is long. All time is brief when given in exchange for Your eternity, yet a single day or even a single hour is very long for one who does not know—and fears—if they are to offend You. 

O free will <note>original: *libre aluedrio*. St. Teresa refers to the human capacity to choose, which she views as a burden if not directed toward God.</note>, so much a slave to your own liberty unless you live nailed <note>original: *enclauado*. This term evokes being "nailed" or "fastened" to the Cross, suggesting a soul that is fixed firmly in its devotion.</note> by the fear and love of Him who created you! 

Oh, when will that happy day come when you will find yourself drowned in that infinite sea of the highest truth, where you will no longer be free to sin,

->OF THE SOUL TO GOD. 119<-

Blessed are those who are
written in the book of this life.

Hope in God, for even now I

and my God. Perhaps the day
will come when my glory <note>original: *mi gloria*. This is a biblical idiom often found in the Psalms (e.g., Psalm 16:9, Psalm 30:12) where "glory" refers to the human tongue or the spirit specifically when it is lifted up in the act of praising God.</note> sings to Him,

but in the meantime, my strength
shall be found in hope and silence <note>A direct reference to Isaiah 30:15: "In quietness and in confidence [hope] shall be your strength."</note>. I would rather

all creatures <term>creatures: In early modern theology, "creatures" (*criaturas*) refers to all created things—human, animal, or material—as distinct from the Creator.</term> and all their worldly
goods that must come to an end. Do not
forsake me, Lord, for in You
I hope; let my hope not be
put to shame: let me serve You always;
and do with me what You will.

### COUNSELS OF ST. TERESA. 121

To speak to everyone with moderate joy.

Never to mock anything.

Never to reprimand anyone without discretion and humility, and an internal sense of one’s own unworthiness <note>original: *confusión propria*. In Teresian spirituality, "confusion" is not a lack of clarity, but a profound sense of one's own smallness and faults before God, which prevents a person from judging others harshly.</note>.

To adapt oneself to the temperament <note>original: *complisión*. Referring to the "humors" or the natural psychological and physical disposition of a person.</note> of the one with whom you are dealing: with the joyful, be joyful; and with the sad, be sad; in short, to become all things to all people, in order to win them all <note>This is a reference to the Apostle Paul’s words in 1 Corinthians 9:22, a common theme in monastic life emphasizing charity and adaptability for the sake of the spiritual well-being of others.</note>.

Never to speak without thinking it over well, and commending it greatly to our Lord; so that one does not speak of anything that might displease Him.

Never to make excuses for oneself, except in cases of very justifiable cause.

Never to say anything of one's own that is worthy of praise, such as regarding one’s knowledge <term>knowledge: *sciencia* in the original, referring to formal education or intellectual learning, which was often restricted or viewed with suspicion for women during the Counter-Reformation.</term>, virtues, or lineage; unless there is hope that it will be beneficial; and then, let it be done with humility, and with the consideration that those are gifts from the hand of God.

122 **COUNSELS OF ST. TERESA**

Never to exaggerate things much; but rather to state what one feels with moderation.

In all talks and conversations, always mix in some spiritual matters; and by doing this, idle words and gossip <note>original: *murmuraciones*. This refers to the common monastic vice of backbiting or speaking ill of others behind their backs.</note> will be avoided.

Never to affirm anything without first knowing it to be true.

Never to interfere by giving one's opinion on all things, unless it is requested, or charity demands it <note>This reflects the Teresian emphasis on "discreet silence," where one speaks only when it serves a direct purpose of love or necessity.</note>.

When anyone speaks of spiritual things, listen to them with humility and as a student <note>original: *discípulo*. St. Teresa frequently emphasized that even the most advanced spiritual seekers should remain "teachable."</note>; and take for yourself the good that they say.

To your superior and confessor, reveal all your temptations, imperfections, and resistances <note>original: *repugnancias*. In ascetic theology, this refers to internal aversions or a lack of will toward spiritual duties or obedience.</note>, so that they may give you counsel and a remedy to overcome them.

Not to be outside the cell, nor to leave without cause; and upon leaving, to ask <note>The text breaks off here, likely continuing with "to ask for permission" from a superior, a standard rule of monastic enclosure.</note>

->TO HER NUNS. 125<-

...the Lord may grant you in prayer.

Always flee from <term>singularity: the act of drawing attention to oneself or acting differently from the rest of the group</term> as much as is possible; for it is a great evil for the community.

Read the ordinances and the rule of your religious order many times; and observe them in truth.

In all created things, behold the providence of God and His wisdom; and in all of them, praise Him.

Detach your heart from all things: and seek, and you shall find God.

Never show outward devotion that is not present within; but you may well hide your devotion. Do not show your internal devotion, except in cases of great necessity.

"My secret is mine," <note>original: *Mi secreto para mi*. This phrase, originally from Isaiah 24:16 (*secretum meum mihi*), was a favorite of St. Bernard and St. Francis to emphasize that the private intimacy between God and the soul should be kept private rather than boasted about.</note> says St. Francis and St. Bernard.

Regarding food, whether it is well or poorly cooked, do not complain; remembering the gall and vinegar <note>A reference to the bitter substances offered to Jesus during his Crucifixion, used here as a reminder to practice patience and asceticism regarding physical comforts.</note> of Jesus Christ.

<summary>St. Teresa advises her nuns to avoid standing out from the community, to study their religious rules, and to find God in all created things. <meta>This page continues the "Avisos" or spiritual counsels of St. Teresa of Avila, moving from general rules of the order and interior prayer to specific behavioral guidelines for communal life, focusing on conduct during meals and interactions with authority figures.</meta>

126 COUNSELS OF ST. TERESA

At the table <note>the *refectory*, or communal dining hall, where silence was traditionally observed</note>, speak to no one, nor lift your eyes to look at another.

Consider the table of heaven, and the food served there, which is God, and the guests, who are the angels; lift your eyes to that table, desiring to find yourself there.

In the presence of your superior—in whom you ought to see Jesus Christ—never speak, except for what is necessary, and with great reverence.

Never do anything that you could not do in the presence of everyone.

Do not make comparisons between one person and another, for it is an odious <note>in this context, "odious" implies something that breeds resentment, favoritism, or discord within a community</note> thing.

When you are reprimanded for something, receive it with interior and exterior humility; and pray to God for the person who reprimanded you.

When a superior <term>commands: the vow of obedience required a nun to follow the instructions of her Prioress as if they came from God</term> a thing: do not say that another superior commands the opposite; instead, believe that they all have holy intentions, and obey what is commanded of you.

<summary>St. Teresa provides instructions on communal discipline, advising nuns to maintain silence at meals, avoid making comparisons between sisters, and practice perfect obedience to superiors.</summary>
<keywords>refectory, humility, obedience, religious superior, communal life, St. Teresa of Avila, discipline</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Avisos" or spiritual counsels of St. Teresa of Avila. ->TO HER NUNS. 127<-

In matters that neither concern nor affect her, she should not be curious to speak of them or ask about them.

Keep her past life present in her mind to weep for it, as well as her present lukewarmness <note>original: *tibieza*, a spiritual state of indifference or lack of fervor that the soul must actively combat</note>, and the distance she has yet to travel from here to heaven, so as to live with fear <note>this refers to "holy fear," a reverent awe of God that leads to spiritual growth rather than paralyzing terror</note>, which is the cause of great blessings.

What those of the house <note>the other sisters or members within the religious community</note> tell her, she should always do, unless it is contrary to obedience; and she should respond to them with humility and gentleness.

She should not ask for any special thing regarding food or clothing, except in cases of great necessity.

Never cease to humble herself and practice <term>mortification: the spiritual discipline of denying the physical self or the ego to strengthen the soul’s focus on God</term> in all things until death.

Always accustom herself to performing many acts of love, for they set the soul on fire and soften it.

Perform acts of all the other virtues.

<summary>St. Teresa provides guidelines for the internal and external life of a nun, emphasizing the avoidance of idle curiosity, the necessity of constant humility, and the importance of performing frequent "acts" of love to maintain spiritual fervor.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Avisos" or spiritual counsels of St. Teresa of Avila. The previous page concluded a series of instructions on humility and acts of love; this page focuses on the dedication of daily actions to God, the moderation of temperament, and the proper mindset for receiving the Eucharist.</meta>

->128 COUNSELS OF ST. TERESA<-

Offer all things to the Eternal Father together with the merits of His Son Jesus Christ.

Be gentle with everyone, and rigorous with oneself.

On the feast days of the Saints, reflect upon their virtues, and ask the Lord to grant them to her.

Take great care with the nightly examination <note>This refers to the *Examen of Conscience*, a daily spiritual exercise of reviewing one's thoughts, words, and deeds to identify sins and moments of grace.</note>.

On the day she receives communion, her prayer should be to see that, being so wretched, she is to receive God; and the prayer of that night should be that she has received Him.

Never, when serving as a superior <note>original: *superior*, the nun in charge of the community or a specific department</note>, should she reprimand anyone with anger, but only when it has passed; and in this way, the reprimand will be of benefit.

Strive greatly for perfection and devotion; and do all things with them.

Exercise oneself much in the fear of the Lord, which brings the soul to compun- <note>original: *compun-*, likely the beginning of *compunción* or compunction; a state of heartfelt regret for sins that leads to a closer relationship with the divine.</note>

<summary>St. Teresa offers guidance on maintaining a disciplined internal life, advising nuns to be strict with themselves but kind to others, while providing specific instructions for prayer during the reception of the Eucharist.</summary>
<keywords>Eternal Father, Jesus Christ, gentleness, hagiography, Examen of Conscience, Holy Communion, leadership, anger, spiritual perfection, fear of the Lord, compunction, St. Teresa of Avila</keywords>

--- Page 333 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 128, completing the thought on the "fear of the Lord" leading the soul to a state of repentance. The text continues St. Teresa’s "Avisos" (Counsels) to her cloistered nuns, transitioning from general temperament to specific advice on spiritual direction, the intercession of saints, and resisting temptation during spiritual dryness.</meta>

->TO HER NUNS. 129<-

...pricked by compunction and humbled. <note>original: *pungida y humillada*. This completes the sentence from the previous page: "the fear of the Lord, which brings the soul to compunction."</note>

Observe carefully how quickly people change, and how little trust is to be placed in them; and so, cling firmly to God, who does not change.

Strive to discuss the matters of your soul with a spiritual and learned <note>original: *docto*. Teresa famously preferred "learned" confessors—those with formal theological training—over those who were merely pious, believing that ignorance in a director could lead a soul into error.</note> confessor, to whom you should communicate them and follow in all things.

Each time you receive communion, ask God for some gift, in light of the great mercy with which He has come to your poor soul.

Although you may have many Saints as advocates, let St. Joseph <note>St. Teresa of Avila was a primary figure in reviving the cult of St. Joseph in the West; she credited him with her physical healing and named her first reformed convent after him.</note> be your particular one, for he obtains much from God.

In times of sadness and turmoil, do not abandon the good works of prayer and penance you were accustomed to doing; for the devil seeks to disturb you so that you might leave them. Instead, do more than you were accustomed, and you will see how quickly the Lord favors you.

m <note>This "m" is a catchword, a printer’s mark at the bottom of a page to help the binder ensure the pages are in the correct order.</note>

<note>A faint circular library stamp is visible on the bottom right margin, marking the volume as part of a historical collection.</note>

<summary>St. Teresa advises her nuns to trust in the unchanging nature of God over the fickle nature of people, to seek out educated spiritual directors, and to increase their devotion specifically when facing sadness or demonic temptation.</summary>
<keywords>St. Teresa of Avila, spiritual direction, learned confessors, Holy Communion, St. Joseph, spiritual dryness, demonic temptation, constancy of God</keywords>

--- Page 334 ---
<meta>This page concludes the series of "Avisos" (Counsels) by St. Teresa of Avila, continuing from the advice regarding spiritual direction and the intercession of St. Joseph on page 129. The text shifts from external guidance to internal discipline and the soul's focus on eternity.</meta>

->130 ADVICE OF ST. TERESA TO HER NUNS.<-

Do not share your temptations and imperfections with those in the house who are the least advanced <note>original: *desaprouechadas*. This refers to nuns who have made little spiritual progress or are currently "unprofitable" in their discipline. Teresa warns that sharing struggles with the spiritually weak can discourage them and provide no remedy for the speaker.</note>, for you will do harm to yourself and to the others; instead, share them with those who are most perfect.

Remember that you have but one soul; nor are you to die more than once; nor have you more than one brief life, which is your own particular concern; nor is there more than one glory, and that one is eternal. If you remember this, you will let go <note>original: *daràs de mano*. Literally "to give from the hand," meaning to renounce, disregard, or set aside worldly concerns.</note> of many things.

Let your desire be to see God; your fear, whether you might lose Him; your sorrow, that you do not yet enjoy Him; and your joy, in that which can lead you to Him; and you shall live in great peace.

->THANKS BE TO GOD. <note>original: *DEO GRATIAS*.</note><-

<note>A circular library stamp is visible in the bottom left corner belonging to the *Bibliothèque de l'Arsenal* (Library of the Arsenal) in Paris. It features a central emblem of an eagle with its wings spread, surrounded by the library's name.</note>

<summary>This page concludes St. Teresa’s spiritual counsels, emphasizing the importance of seeking counsel only from spiritually mature peers and maintaining a singular focus on the soul's eternal destiny.</summary>
<keywords>St. Teresa of Avila, spiritual direction, memento mori, asceticism, monastic life, salvation, Bibliothèque de l'Arsenal</keywords>
